What §8 also gives a file-type mode, the defaults it sets (soft wrap, indentation), still waits for M1: your settings by path stand.
1105. **API (die-core):** `TextBuf::redecorate(old, new)`: remove some decorations and add others as one change, with rows laid out again and cursors moved out of hidden text once, after all of it. `decorate` and `undecorate` do that each time, which is right for a fold and quadratic for a file's markup: 5,000 hides took 184 ms one by one and 2.2 ms as one change, and 20,000 take 11 ms (a debug-profile probe, not kept; one by one they'd take about 3 s). `decorate` is now `redecorate` with one, so its signature lost a `mut` and nothing else of it changed; all or none, as a transaction is: a bad range anywhere changes nothing.
1106. **API (die-core):** `Ctx::on_edited(f)`, the edits' hook of contract §5 ("edits (after the fact)"), which nothing had needed yet: `f(ctx, buffer)` runs from the top of the loop for each text buffer whose text has changed, once the keys and calls that were ready have run. So it's once for a macro's hundred edits, and it hears every edit whoever made it: a key, undo, a timer's reload of a file that changed on disk, another extension. Its calls are part of the turn that made the edits: timers and output get no further batch until they've run, so a repeat that edits can't keep the frame waiting through them (the review found my first cut could: 1122). A buffer killed before its turn isn't told of. It says that the text changed; `edits_since` says how (C15). It costs one comparison for each text buffer each time the loop runs out of work, and nothing else until someone subscribes. `kill` queues its hooks through the same helper, which took 13 lines out of `workspace.rs`. One more line of behaviour changed with it: a mode's `on_enable` for a buffer killed before it ran is dropped quietly, where it logged that the buffer was gone, since a file-type mode makes that an ordinary thing (a file opened and closed in one turn).
1107. **For veto:** the budget. die-core 15,775 → 15,859, its limit 15,780 → 15,860: text and undo 1,448 → 1,469 (limit 1,470), ext, dispatch and rt 1,647 → 1,722 (1,725), files 833 → 834 (835), workspace 1,404 → 1,391 (limit left at 1,405). 84 lines of core for three pieces of the contract that folding (manifold) and a highlighter will stand on too. die-markdown is 1,866 lines, uncapped; die-dev 241 → 275.
1108. **For veto:** the parser is pulldown-cmark 0.13.6, pinned, with its default features off (no HTML renderer, no `getopts`): the CommonMark parser rustdoc uses, with GFM's tables, strikethrough, task lists, footnotes, alerts and YAML front matter, and byte ranges for everything it reports, which is what hiding markup needs. A hand-written parser was the alternative: more lines than the rest of the extension, and wrong at CommonMark's corners for years. It's pure Rust; its build script does nothing unless a feature that generates its own tests is on, and `cargo xtask clean-build` passes with it. It stays behind `plan.rs`, which knows nothing of the editor.
1109. What rendered shows; using-die.md's "## markdown" has the table. Headings without their `#`s; emphasis, strong and strikethrough without their marks; inline code without backticks; a link as its text; an image as `‹image: alt›`; bullets as `•`, `◦`, `▪` by depth, numbers kept, task boxes as `☐` and `☑` where the bullet was; a quote's `>` as `▎`, and GitHub's five alerts by their title; a rule as a line across the text; code blocks as boxes (1111); tables aligned (1112); front matter hidden (1113); `\*` and `&amp;` as the character; HTML and footnote labels as written, dimmed. Paragraphs keep their line breaks and blank lines stay: nothing is reflowed, so a row on screen is a line of the file, wrapped as it wraps raw. Every glyph the view draws that the file hasn't got (`• ◦ ▪ ▎ ─ ═ │ ├ ┼ ┤ ☐ ☑ ‹ ›`) is in Ioskeley Mono Term, checked against the font file through Core Text.
1110. **For veto:** no larger headings. The cell grid has one size of letter (D6, D11), and scaled text runs are scope's open question 2, so headings differ by weight, underline and brightness: h1 bold over a double underline, h2 over a single one (your stylesheet's `border-bottom` on both), h3 bold, h4 to h6 each dimmer. Whether that reads as a heading at a glance is yours to say once you've seen it; if it doesn't, question 2 has its asker.
1111. **For veto:** a code block is a filled box, your stylesheet's `pre`. A face on text stops at each line's end, so the box is made: the block's face on every row, and virtual spaces after each line out to the longest, or to the text's width, where a longer line wraps and gets none. The opening fence's row shows the language, dimmed, and the closing fence's is a blank row of the box, so the block has as many rows as it has lines in the file and `5j` goes where it went raw. Code is one colour: DIE has no highlighter. An indented block is a box with no fence rows.
1112. Tables. Columns are aligned whatever the source's spacing, with no virtual text: each `|`, with the whitespace round it, is hidden under a replacement that carries the padding owed to the cells on either side and a `│`, and the delimiter row becomes `├───┼───┤`. `:--`, `:-:` and `--:` align. A table wider than the text gives up width, widest column first, until the header fits; a cell that still doesn't fit runs its own row over. A table whose rows pulldown reads differently from its header (a cell too many: an unescaped `|` in a code span) is left as written rather than half drawn, as is one with a tab in a cell.
1113. **For veto:** front matter is hidden, since your preview's export strips it (`user/markdown-strip-frontmatter`). It's a block at the very top only; `Options::front_matter` shows it dimmed instead. In a scrib note the title and tags are there, so you may want it shown: it's one line in your config.
1114. **For veto:** links. In the rendered view `<CR>` on a link follows it (`markdown-follow-link`, in a keymap over the link's text, as contract §9 has buttons): `#heading` goes to that heading, by GitHub's rule for anchors; a file opens in this window through `Ctx::open_target`, at its top, at its heading if the link names one, or at a line (`notes.md:12`, as `die notes.md:12` reads it), so `C-o` comes back, and rendered if this view was and it's markdown; anything with a scheme, a mail address in angle brackets (as `mailto:`), and a picture, a PDF or an HTML file, goes to `Options::opener`, by default `xdg-open` where there is one and `open` otherwise, tried in that order since an extension can't ask which platform it's on. So a page is your browser's, as you said web content should be. Off a link `<CR>` is die-vim's. Raw, there's no keymap on the text, and `:markdown-follow-link` follows the link the cursor is in. A link to a file that isn't there says so and opens nothing, where `:e` would make the buffer; a heading the file hasn't got is said, and the file opens at its top. A link to a file already open goes to its top too, as a browser would, not to where its cursor was. In a buffer with no file, `#heading` keeps no place for `C-o`: core keeps one only through `open_target`, which goes by a file's name.
1115. **For veto:** the faces. Eighteen, `markdown-h1` to `markdown-h6`, `-bold`, `-italic`, `-strike`, `-code`, `-code-block`, `-link`, `-quote`, `-marker`, `-rule`, `-table-header`, `-meta`, `-alert`, each on a base face (`heading`, `bold`, `shadow`, `highlight`, `link`), so any theme draws them. mono-dark styles each from the stylesheet your preview uses (`markdown-xwidget/resources/mono-dark.css`), on the same palette: links red, bold and underlined; strong bright; em and quotes `#888888` italic; code `#888888` on `#252525`, and a block's box `#252525`; rules, the quote bar and a table's borders in the border's colour. Where it departs from the stylesheet: h4 to h6 dim by level where a browser shrinks them (1110); bullets and numbers are `#888888`, not the text's colour, so the text leads; a table's header has no background and its rows no stripes, since a row's padding is drawn by its borders.
1116. What the view follows, and what it keeps. It's planned again when the buffer's text changes under it (`on_edited`: `:e!`, or a reload from disk, so a file something else is writing stays rendered as it changes), and when the text's width does (`on_resize`, and after each command for `\z` and for another window leaving the buffer), since rules and boxes are as wide as the text. A new plan swaps only the decorations between the first that differs and the last: core has moved the rest through the edits, so a line changed in a long file swaps a handful. Which buffers are rendered is in the session (`save_session`), each with the read-only setting it had before the view, so `:rebuild` shows them as they were and raw gives the setting back; a buffer killed, or saved under another kind of name, leaves it.
1117. What it costs, measured on the harness with die-vim, test profile: rendering using-die.md (79 KB, 3,613 decorations) takes 3.2 ms, and this log (479 KB, 13,050 decorations) 11.6 ms; a plan of this log is about 4 ms, a frame of it rendered 0.8 ms, `G` 1.6 ms, raw again 2 ms (the review's numbers, an optimized build). A raw buffer costs nothing, and a rendered one a comparison after each command. The one cliff: core moves every decoration once for each change in a transaction (`map_all`, older than this work), so a file rewritten in 40,000 places while it's rendered takes about 2 s to follow, where raw takes 50 ms. With the view read-only that's a reload's cost only, and I left it: it wants decorations moved once for a transaction, which is core's.
1118. How it was built. I wrote core's three pieces, the mode, the commands, the session and the config's side; a builder agent wrote the planner from a brief, in its own worktree, and checked pulldown's ranges by probe before leaning on them. Its departures from the brief, each sound: tables fit to the width (1112); a first cell with no pipe before it pads on its right; front matter only at byte 0; a lone `\r` ends a line, as pulldown reads it; widths capped at 4,096. One thing it flagged I fixed: a blank line inside an indented block, or inside a fenced block in a list or a quote, hasn't the indentation the box starts after, so its row of the box started at the left edge. It now has the paper as far as the block's column and the box from there (two pieces of virtual text at the newline, the first in the `default` face), so the box's left edge is straight; the view keeps pieces that share a position in order when it swaps only some.
1119. Tests. die-core's `markup` (4): a file-type mode comes with its files and goes with another name; `on_edited` says each changed buffer once, after what was ready, and not one killed since; a slow repeat that edits still hands the loop back once its edit is told of; `redecorate` swaps as one change, and a bad range changes nothing. die-markdown's `plan` (15): one for each family of 1109 against `Plan::preview`, and one that any text has a plan core can take: every `.md` under `docs/`, each cut at 200 places, and about 80 nasty strings cut at every character, with hides sorted, apart, on character boundaries, and no newline in anything shown. Its `view` (4), on the harness with die-vim: the key shows a file rendered and raw again over the same text, and is no key in a `.txt`; the view is for reading (`x`, `~`, `dd`, typing and `u` are each refused and change nothing), follows a reload from disk and a narrower window, and shows what was written raw; a restart brings it back, keeps as the same session, and raw then takes your edits; a link goes to a heading, a file, a line, the top of a file whose heading is gone, or the opener, a script that writes down what it was given, so no test opens a browser. die-dev's `markdown` (1): through your config, `\m` renders in mono-dark's colours and is undefined in a buffer that isn't markdown; and its theme test holds mono-dark to styling every markdown face. No timing limits.
1120. Left for later (**for veto**): pictures (M3's images: `‹image: alt›` until then); larger headings (1110); colours in code (a highlighter); editing in the rendered view, by showing the cursor's line raw as Vim's `concealcursor` does (1101); opening rendered by itself (1102); following a link with the pointer; link reference definitions, which show as written; math, mermaid and what else only your browser draws; a table wider than the text, which wraps; two windows of different widths on one rendered buffer, which is planned for the narrower; and the defaults a file-type mode sets (M1).
1121. **For veto:** nobody has seen it on screen. It was checked as cells and faces on the harness, and against real files as text; no test DIE drew it and nothing went on your screen. The first look is yours: `\m` in any `.md` after your `:rebuild`. What only a look tells: whether headings read as headings without size, whether `#252525` boxes on `#1a1a1a` read as code, how red, bold, underlined links sit in a paragraph of mono text, and whether a view you can't type in is the view you meant, or you'd rather edit in it with the cursor's line raw (1101).
1122. The review, by an independent agent on 9ff9292, with a reproduction for each finding; ten findings, two major, all read against the code and each either fixed or held here. **Major, fixed:** editing at the edge of hidden markup changed the markup too (1101: the view is read-only now, and its tests hold `x`, `~`, `dd`, typing and `u` to changing nothing). **Major, fixed, in core:** my `on_edited` told of edits from the place in `step` where the loop is about to hand back, after the turn's limits on timers and output were reset, so a repeat slower than its period that edits text got batch after batch with no frame between, once anything subscribed: 300 firings in one `run_ready` in the review's probe. Nothing shipped does that, and die-markdown always subscribes, so it was one slow timer away. The telling is now before those limits are reset, and a test holds it. **Minor, fixed:** a width change the view missed when another window left the buffer (it now looks at every rendered buffer after a command, not the focused one); `<you@example.com>` followed as a file (it's `mailto:`); `notes.md:12` taken for a scheme (a scheme has no dot, and the name is read as `die` reads it); a link to a heading its file hasn't got loaded the file and showed nothing (the file opens at its top, with a word); a killed buffer's id reaching an `on_edited` subscriber (1106). **Held, and said in using-die.md:** `gg` can't reach hidden front matter, so `ggyG` leaves it out; `#heading` in a buffer with no file keeps no jump (1114); many changes at once under a rendered view are slow to follow (1117). **Sentences that weren't so**, corrected in using-die.md and the contract: "the cursor stays where it was" (from a mark that gets hidden it moves to the text after it, and stays there), "`j` and `k` move by the rows you see" (by lines, as raw), "every key works, editing included", "before the frame that shows them" (not for `*messages*`, which changes at a frame, nor past the host's limit on a long run of keys). What it checked and found sound: `decorate` through `redecorate` behaves as it did, `on_edited` is neither missed nor doubled, file-type modes through open, restore and `:saveas` both ways, the swap in `View::render` (the buffer's decorations equal the plan after every render, and nothing foreign is removed), the session with and without the extension, and the planner's picture of every `docs/*.md`.
1123. The gate on this tree: `cargo fmt --all --check`, `cargo xtask lint` (14 crates), `cargo xtask budget` (die-core 15,859 of 15,860; die-markdown 1,866, uncapped; die-dev 275), `cargo xtask sources` (297 files), `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings`, `cargo xtask cross-check` (both Linux targets), and under `DIE_UNSEEN=1` `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast`: 1,927 passed, 40 ignored, none failed, die-render's among them. `cargo xtask clean-build` passed before the review's fixes, which added no dependency. `cargo xtask api-diff`: `ModeSpec::files`, `Ctx::on_edited` and `TextBuf::redecorate` added, `TextBuf::decorate`'s `deco` no longer `mut`, and die-vim's grammar unchanged. Not run: the ignored tests of `ci --full`, `ci --linux`, `mutate`, and S1's on-screen checks, which wait for a run you start.

**die-markdown's page: the rendered view drawn as GitHub draws a README (2026-11-18)**

1124. What you asked, after the first look at 1100's view: "it does work but the styling kinda sucks - im hoping for rendering like on github using the graphical features we have access to in DIE". The graphical features are M3a's pixel buffers (981), so the rendered view is now a page drawn in pixels. It's a custom surface, `NAME *page*` (kind `markdown-page`), which `\m` shows in the file's window and `\m` on it leaves. It has the system's proportional font, real heading sizes, rounded code panels with syntax colours, tables with a grid and striped rows, drawn task boxes, alert bars and icons, and pictures. All of it is in die-markdown, which rasterizes the page itself with the whiteboard's tiny-skia into one pixel buffer per window. No line of die-core or die-render changed.
1125. **For veto:** the page is a second buffer beside the file's, not the file's buffer under decorations. A pixel buffer can only be placed over a custom surface's whole area (§11: `Hide` with a placement waits for M3), and proportional text can't live in the cell grid, so there was no other way to draw it. Consequences: the page has no cursor, no search, no selection and no yank. It's a picture of the file, and raw is where you edit, as 1101 already had it. The file's cursor and undo wait where they were. 1101's view in place stays as the fallback where the host shows no pixels (Linux), where no font can be read, and with `page: false`; its tests run unchanged with the option off.
1126. **For veto:** the page is GitHub's geometry in your theme's colours. The geometry comes from GitHub's stylesheet (the `github_css/dark.css` your preview carries): 16 px on 24 px lines, h1 to h6 at 32, 24, 20, 16, 14 and 13.6 px semibold, 16 px between blocks and 24 above a heading, a 980 CSS px column with 45 px of padding (15 under 768). The colours are read from the `markdown-*` faces, which mono-dark styles from your `mono-dark.css`. `palette: GithubDark` or `GithubLight` gives GitHub's own. Where GitHub's sheet and yours disagree on more than colour, I took GitHub's: links at normal weight and underlined, in your red; quotes upright, not italic; code blocks without a border; tables sized to their content, with an unshaded header; a rule as a 4 px bar. Your sheet's colour roles stay: strong bright, em `#888888`, struck `#555555`, headings `#eeeeee` with h6 muted.
1127. **For veto:** die-dev's mono-dark changed in three faces. `markdown-code`'s background is now `#2e2e2e`, the theme's highlight grey, so inline code reads as a pill on a striped table row, as GitHub's does. `markdown-h4` and `-h5` are now bright like h1 to h3, since the page has sizes to tell them apart. The view in place shows all three changes. A die-dev test holds the palette read from mono-dark's faces equal to the typesetter's `Palette::mono_dark()`, field by field.
1128. **For veto:** fonts. The page reads the system's fonts at run time, which on this Mac are `SFNS.ttf`, `SFNSItalic.ttf` and `SFNSMono.ttf`: San Francisco, GitHub's first choice on a Mac. They're read with ttf-parser 0.25.1 at the weight and optical size CoreText uses (wght 400 and 590, opsz from the CSS size, never under 17), with CoreText's `trak` tracking and GPOS pair kerning. The kerning is read from the table's bytes, because ttf-parser's own reader drops some pairs. Measured against CoreText, advances agree to the font unit and line widths to 0.03 CSS px. Fallbacks are probed in order: Helvetica Neue, Helvetica and Arial, Menlo, Apple Symbols, Arial Unicode and Hiragino for symbols and CJK. Linux's DejaVu, Noto and Liberation paths are written but untried. DIE's own Ioskeley Mono is the last resort. No font is shipped: an extension can't embed a file (`cargo xtask sources`) or choose paths by platform (the lint), so every path is probed when the page is drawn. ttf-parser was already in Cargo.lock as fontdue's reader, so the lock still holds 255 packages; the workspace pin adds the features a variable font needs, `gvar-alloc` among them, since without it SF has no outlines for ASCII.
1129. **For veto:** the weight of the letters. Glyphs are filled at twice the size with tiny-skia and averaged down. Plain coverage drew text about a fifth lighter than a Mac browser draws GitHub, because a browser smooths fonts and DIE's own text doesn't (`die-render`'s text has smoothing off). So the page grows each glyph by an amount that depends on its colour's lightness and its size, fitted against CoreText's smoothed ink over 13 kinds of text, three palettes and three scales. The page now carries 95 to 106 % of CoreText's ink, and 99 to 101.5 % at 2x. One constant in `sheet.rs` gives the plain weight back. At 2x the page's text is heavier than the editor's beside it, as a browser's is.
1130. **For veto:** hairlines (the rule under h1 and h2, a table's grid, kbd caps) are one CSS pixel in whole device pixels: 1 at 1x and 1.5x, 2 at 2x, as a browser draws them. One device pixel at 2x read as spidery beside GitHub.
1131. **For veto:** a table too wide for the column. GitHub scrolls it sideways, and a picture can't scroll a piece of itself. So the table runs into the right margin, up to the window's padding; then its cells give up padding down to 4 px; only then are words cut, in the column with the longest unbreakable piece first. A line may end after a hyphen (unless a digit follows), a slash, a `?` in an address, a zero-width space, and between ideographs, kana and hangul.
1132. **For veto:** syntax colours, which neither the brief nor 1100 had. Your preview colours code with highlight.js's `grayscale-dark` (init.el's `markdown-xwidget-code-block-theme`), and GitHub colours a README's code, so a page without them read as neither. `typeset/syntax.rs` is a highlighter of its own, with no dependency. One tokenizer is driven by a table for each of some thirty languages, chosen by the fence's first word (the guide lists them). It runs a line at a time, carrying what's open across lines and never going back, at about 145 MB/s. A block with no language, or one it doesn't know, is plain, as on GitHub. mono-dark's colours are grayscale-dark's greys by its own mapping. Its comments are lifted from `#525252` to `#545454` to reach 2:1 on the `#252525` panel; your preview draws them on `#101010`. The GitHub palettes take GitHub's prettylights colours. A theme sets them through fourteen new faces, `markdown-code-keyword` to `markdown-code-heading`. `-comment`, `-inserted`, `-deleted` and `-heading` stand on `shadow`, `success`, `error` and `heading`, so a theme that styles only the base faces still gets dim comments and green and red diffs. Left out: diff line backgrounds, bold or italic in code, embedded languages (a `<script>`'s body, `${}` in a template string).
1133. **For veto:** the page's keys, as using-die.md's table has them. They're the extension's own kind keymap: `j`/`k` scroll 60 CSS px, `<C-d>`/`<C-u>` half a window, `<C-f>` `<Space>` `<C-b>` a window less an eighth, `gg` `G`, `]]` `[[`, `<Tab>`/`<S-Tab>` ring a link, `<CR>` and a click follow it, `<C-o>`/`H` back and `L` forward, digits for a count, and `\m` (die-dev binds it in the page's keymap beside the mode's). Forward isn't `<C-i>`, because core folds `<C-i>` into `<Tab>` in every keymap. `<S-Space>` can't be written in key notation, so the forward command reads Shift off the key event. A textless surface inherits none of die-vim's keys, so `:`, `ZZ`, `ZQ`, `<C-^>` and the `<C-w>` table are bound by command name, the third copy of that table after the whiteboard's and the jellyfish's. The wheel and the trackpad scroll by pixels, with the fraction carried.
1134. Links on a page. An anchor finds a heading by GitHub's rule, in any case; `fn-`/`fnref-` a footnote and each reference to it; an `<a id>` or `<a name>`. A place inside a closed `<details>` opens it first, as a browser does. A markdown file opens as its page, at the heading or line the link names. Any other file opens as text. A path from `/` is from the repository's top (the nearest `.git` above), for links and pictures alike. The rest goes to your opener. Every follow keeps the place it left in the window's trail, which outlives the page, so `<C-o>` comes back across pages. A file opened as text has the page's own file shown first at the line the page's top showed, since core's jump list keeps no place on a surface, so `<C-o>` and `<C-^>` from the text come back there and `\m` brings the page back.
1135. **For veto:** a page in two windows is a view in each, as two browser windows on one page are: each has its own pixel buffer, its own layout for its width, its own place, its own ringed link and its own trail, and a window that leaves a page another still shows keeps its place for its return. Equal widths share one layout. The first build had one picture for all of a page's windows, and a check that composited what each window was presented found a narrow window's lines cut off and `G` in a short window stopping short. The cost is a layout for each width on each edit.
1136. **For veto:** a page no window shows is killed when the command ends, and its file keeps where you were (the source byte, the ringed link, the `<details>` you opened), so the next `\m`, a `<C-o>` or a link into it starts there. Otherwise hidden `*page*` buffers piled up in the Buffer List and came back at every restart. The session keeps a file's page as `{"page": byte, "shown": bool}` and a put-away place as a number; a session the previous build wrote (a Bool, the view in place) restores as before. Core drops a window whose buffer it didn't keep, so a restart shows the page the focused window showed, in that window, as the whiteboard's board comes back. Other windows on pages come back on another buffer, and their files remember their places.
1137. HTML, as GitHub renders it in a README: `<br>` (a block of them opens space), `<kbd>`, `<sub>`, `<sup>`, `<b>`, `<i>`, `<em>`, `<strong>`, `<code>`, `<a href>`, `<img src alt width height>`, `align="center"` and `"right"` on `<p>`, `<div>` and headings, `id` and `name` as anchors, and `<details>`/`<summary>`. Comments vanish, and other tags vanish with their text kept. Not handled: `<table>`, `<ul>`, `<ol>` (their text is kept, their structure lost), `<picture>`, floats.
1138. Pictures. PNG only, through the png crate the whiteboard already pins, decoded on a job's thread and laid out again when they arrive. A picture is scaled to the column and never past its own size at the display's scale. A remote picture (README badges), another format, or a missing file shows its alternative text behind a small picture mark, and `<CR>` sends it to your opener. A large picture's first paint scales it on the main thread: about 30 ms for 4000 × 3000.
1139. What it costs, on this Mac (M5 Pro), release, a Retina window of 160 × 47 cells, measured through the harness and its fake pixel sink. `\m` shows using-die.md (80 KB) in about 18 ms and this log (480 KB) in about 29 ms, to the first present. A `j` takes about 2 ms and a `<C-d>` 1.5 to 3.2 ms. An edit with the page shown takes 4 ms and 13 ms, and 7 and 26 ms with the page in two windows of different widths, which lay it out twice. Layout alone is 2 ms and 11 ms. A painted viewport is about 1.2 ms once its glyphs are drawn, and 10 to 13 ms for the first, which draws them. A window's pixel buffer is about 23 MB in core at that size, and the host holds about four times as much again. The highlighter adds nothing to a file without code blocks, and about 2 ms to a 200 KB block.
1140. **For veto:** the budget. die-core and die-render are untouched. die-markdown is 13,385 lines, uncapped (an extension), up from 1,866. The typesetter is most of that: layout, the document model, fonts and kerning, the highlighter, paint. die-dev goes from 275 to 298. The Cargo.toml pin for ttf-parser is the only new line outside `extensions/`.
1141. How it was built. A reading round mapped the surface and pixel API, the whiteboard, the host's renderer, the old die-markdown and GitHub's geometry, and spiked the font path with pictures. I froze the typesetter's interface (`typeset.rs`) behind a stand-in, and two builders worked at once in their own worktrees: the typesetter, and the page surface against the stand-in. Critics judged the typesetter by rendering PNGs and looking at them, measuring the gaps against the spec with a script, through three lenses: type and rhythm, blocks, and edges. Adversarial reviewers went through the surface. Two rounds of fixes followed, then the merge, a check that composited each window's presented pixels against `page_shot`'s to the pixel (117 frames, none off by a pixel), a third builder for the highlighter, and a last adversarial pass of three checkers, all three of whose verdicts were that it could go to you as it was. Their findings were fixed all the same: a trail and a place per window, a deleted `<details>` that opened the next one, Ruby, JSX and shell-arithmetic colours, front matter only at the top.
1142. Tests. die-markdown's `typeset` has the spec's worked pages to the pixel: heading heights, code blocks of n lines, table rows, the gaps between blocks. Every `docs/*.md` and the samples are laid out and painted at widths from 0 to 3,024 and scales 1, 1.5 and 2, with every painted row opaque, link boxes inside the page, headings in order, and the source map monotone. It also checks kerning against CoreText's widths, a picture from wanted to drawn, details, spacers, anchors, hyphens, ideographs, hairlines at each scale, and colours in a Rust block. `page`, on the harness with the real typesetter: the toggle both ways and the cursor rule; every key's amount; the wheel; the ring from what's in view; clicks; every kind of link; the trail across pages; two windows of unequal width and height, each window's pixels equal to a lone window's; edits, reloads and the place kept; opened details through edits; put-away pages; the session and the old session shape; `page: false`. `view`'s four tests run with the page off. die-dev's `markdown` covers `\m` both ways through your config, the palette equal to mono-dark's, and the view in place in its colours. Mutation runs broke 25 behaviours one at a time, and a test caught each. A fuzz run of 60,000 documents at widths 0 to 3,000 and scales up to 20 found no panic; it isn't committed.
1143. Left for later (**for veto**): search, selection and yank on a page (it would need its own, since copy state draws cells); editing on the page; formats other than PNG, and remote pictures; colour emoji (Apple Color Emoji is 192 MB of PNGs); a shaper for right-to-left text, combining marks and ligatures; math and mermaid; a soft hyphen as a break; the page on Linux (the wgpu ring); a restart that brings back every window on a page (core's surface restorer, M1); a pointer that becomes a hand over a link (no cursor-shape API); the exact position in a page window's status line while a prompt's list is open over two windows on one page (core redraws the status lines then without telling a surface's `status()` which window it's for, so the line says what every window on the page has if they agree, and only the page's name if they don't).
1144. **For veto:** nobody has seen the page on screen. It was checked as the pixels the harness's sink was handed, as PNG files looked at slice by slice, and against CoreText's drawing of the same lines off screen. No browser capture of github.com was made: every "as GitHub" is the stylesheet's numbers and the critics' memory. What only a look tells: whether the weight reads right beside the editor's own text, whether mono-dark's grey code colours are the ones you want on a page, and whether scrolling by trackpad feels smooth. Every OS scroll event is a frame, and nothing coalesces them.
1145. **For veto:** the rule of two (scope §9, D11) has its second user: die-markdown and die-whiteboard both wrap tiny-skia now, and both find DIE's font directory the same way. I didn't lift a shared `die-draw` crate. The lint's D14 rule lets an extension depend on die-core alone, so a shared crate is a change to that rule first. And the two share less than the rule supposes. The whiteboard strokes paths and keeps a canvas it shifts; the page fills glyph masks, rounded panels and scaled pictures into a viewport. The shared part would be the font-directory rule, a premultiplied blit and the PNG decode, about 150 lines. Say if you want it lifted anyway.
1146. The gate on this tree:
- `cargo fmt --all --check`.
- `cargo xtask lint` (14 crates).
- `cargo xtask budget`: die-core 15,859 of 15,860 and die-render untouched; die-markdown 13,385, uncapped; die-dev 298.
- `cargo xtask sources` (311 files).
- `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets -D warnings`.
- `cargo xtask cross-check` (both Linux targets).
- `cargo xtask clean-build` (locked, offline, no C compiler; on the tree before the last pass's fixes, which added no dependency).
- Under `DIE_UNSEEN=1`, `cargo test --workspace --no-fail-fast`: every test binary it had run passed, 89 of them, die-core's, die-vim's, die-markdown's, die-dev's and die-whiteboard's among them. die-render's input tests were still running when you asked to see the page, and I merged then.

Not run: the ignored tests of `ci --full`, `ci --linux`, `mutate`, and S1's on-screen checks.

**die-whiteboard's dial after the reel: line art, a chip and a readout, and an ink arc past the ring (2026-11-18)**

1147. What you asked: "take a look at the interface shown in this reel … especially around the radial selection user interface and how it operates … use it as a basis for how we can provide a good user interface and user experience for the radial selection logic in the whiteboard extension." The reel is "Realtime LLM Vector Steering" (far___behind, MIT Media Lab). Its picker is line art on black, with no panel. A small dotted arc carries the options, and the pointer's direction from the anchor picks one: its name becomes a chip in the one accent, and a line under the arc says what it means. Pushing out past the arc opens a larger solid arc on the same anchor, where the angle picks a value and the distance a strength, with a readout under it (`tone: wondrous 77%`). The dial keeps everything it did: Space or the right button held, the dead zone, the eight sectors with the pen north and then clockwise, what picks and what closes. A stage-one round still changes nothing in the model, the file or the undo log. Now it looks like the reel and has the reel's second stage, which picks the ink by angle and the width by distance.

1148. **For veto:** how it looks. There's no disc and no filled wedge. Under the line art is a scrim of the theme's background. The tool ring is dotted in the dial's grey, with a tick across it between sectors, and the hovered sector's stretch is 3 px thick in the accent. The dead zone is a fainter dotted circle, with a reticle at the centre. A dotted needle runs from the centre to the pointer and ends in the accent's 3 px dot, and a crosshair at a quarter of the accent runs through the pointer, within the dial's own square rather than across the screen as the reel's does. The names are cells outside the ring, lowercase. The hovered name is a chip, the accent behind the background with a cell either side. The readout sits two rows under everything: its key in the dial's grey, its value in the accent, the rest in the default foreground. Line art is in the grey at lower alpha, labels at full grey, and what's active in the accent or the bright grey, which is the reel's hierarchy.

1149. **For veto:** the scrim is held at 0.88 through most of the names, falls to 0.7 at their farthest corner, and is gone a cell past that. The brief had it fading from the centre. A fade like that left ink at half strength under the names, and on a busy board they didn't read. The first renders showed it, with a red stroke through `ellipse` and the readout over an arrow. A board label inside the scrim is drawn faint, as the ink there is dimmed.

1150. **For veto:** the geometry. The ring is 2.5 cell heights out (the disc was 3). Each name hangs off the ring by its end facing the centre: north and south on the centre's column, east and west on its row, the diagonals about 45° out. It moves out a cell at a time until no cell of it is within a tick and 3 px of the ring and it meets no other name. So wherever in a cell the centre falls, no name crosses the ring or another name, and both renderers put them on the same cells (a unit test tries 20 places in a cell). At your 16 pt cells the dial's text spans about 10 rows by 30 columns, the readout included; the disc and its names took about 6 by 18.

1151. Stage two (the brief's design, with the review's two changes, 1166 and 1167). It opens when the pointer goes half a cell past the ring with a tool that draws with ink hovered (pen, rect, ellipse, arrow, line or text, not the eraser or select), on a move after the one that hovered it. Going back inside the ring is stage one again. The ink arc is 5 cells beyond the ring: six segments of 20°, in ink order clockwise, each a hairline in its own ink, with ticks between them and a T at each end. Its first band picks nothing. Past it, the picked ink is drawn 3 px thick. The angle picks the ink, and past either end it's the nearer end's. For all but text the distance picks the width, thin, then medium, then thick, in bands 1.25 cells deep. Faint guide arcs at the bands' edges are marked `2` `4` `8` in the dimmed grey. The needle turns solid, with a tick where it crosses each guide. The readout says `pen: ink 3 · thick`, or `text: ink 5`. Letting go arms the tool as stage one would and sets the ink and width. A left click picks at whichever stage the dial is in. Letting go in stage one, or in the first band, never changes ink or width.

1152. **For veto:** the arc is placed so the armed ink's segment is centred on the tool's direction, the brief's rule, so pushing straight out keeps the ink. It follows that with ink 1, a new board's, the arc runs from 10° before the tool's direction to 110° past it, all on one side. Centring the arc on the tool would look even, but straight out would then pick ink 3 or 4. I kept the rule.

1153. The overshoot, as first built: a quick flick to an inky tool that travelled past 3 cells landed in stage two's thin band, so letting go there set the width to thin. I wrote that only the width changed. The review showed the ink changed too: the armed ink's segment spans 10° either side of the tool's direction and its sector 22.5°, so a flick more than 10° off the axis picked the neighbouring ink, and a selection was restyled with both. A flick done in one move did it as well, since one move could hover the tool and open the arc. Both are fixed (1166, 1167).

1154. **For veto:** the dot's preview. The brief had the dot "at the chosen width, no less than the stage-one dot". The stage-one dot has a 3 px radius, so thin (2 px) and medium (4 px) would come out as the same dot. Instead the dot is the ink at the width itself, at the zoom. When that's smaller than the stage-one dot, it sits on a disc of the same ink at a third (35 %) the stage-one dot's size. Thin and medium differ, and the end is never smaller than in stage one. As first built it was a hairline ring rather than the faint disc; with ink 1 at 1:1 that drew a second reticle at the pointer, and in other inks it read as the reel's hollow pins.

1155. **For veto:** the stage-one names in stage two. The brief had them dim. In the renders, the guides and the arc ran through two or three of them, which tangled the lines with the letters. Now a name the arcs cross gives way, and so does one with a width label within three cells of it, since `select   2` read as one thing. The rest dim. Each renderer counts only what it draws, so in cells only a swatch moves a name. The chosen tool's chip goes past the arc (1168). The needle, its dot and the crosshair draw over the chip's background, under its letters, where the pointer goes through it.

1156. **For veto:** one undo entry for stage two's ink and width together, as one gesture, rather than the two that `2` then `]` make. No entry at all when the selection already has them. `1` on a selection that's already ink 1 still logs an empty change; I left the keys as they are.

1157. **For veto:** the readout. With nothing hovered it says what's armed, dimmed (`pen: ink 1 · medium`, `rect: once`), which the brief left to me. A one-shot tool says `once`, the eraser `on` or `off` (letting go toggles it), and select just its name. Near the area's bottom the readout goes above the dial, and it stays inside the area's sides. The names are cut at the area's edge, as the disc's were.

1158. The faces. The extension registers `whiteboard-dial`, which inherits `shadow` and falls back to the default foreground, and `whiteboard-dial-accent`, which falls back to the theme's ANSI red, as the inks fall back. `die_whiteboard::dial_look(ctx)` resolves them with the background. The surface takes them at each event, as it takes the inks, and a change draws the dial again, which a test with `set_theme` holds. die-dev's mono-dark sets them to `FG_MID` (`#888888`) and `RED` (`#cc4444`), the grey and the one accent it already has, and its theme test knows the two faces.

1159. In cells (Linux, `pixels: false`), the names go round a `+` on the same cells, and the chip is the accent rather than reverse video. Under them is the readout, with what's under the dial dimmed. In stage two the inks are `██` swatches in their colours along the arc, the chip past them, the picked one between `[` `]` in the accent, and the width is in the readout only. The `2 4 8` labels stay out, since cells draw no guides for them to name, and the other names dim but give way only to a swatch.

1160. What it costs. The needle follows the pointer, so every move draws. The pixel renderer keeps what stays while the pointer moves: the scrim, ring, ticks, stretch, ink arc, guides and reticle, drawn over the committed picture under them. A move puts back only the needle's box and the crosshair's two strips from that layer, and draws them again. A change of what the dial says draws the layer again. tiny-skia's radial gradient cost 1.3 ms for your scrim alone, so the scrim is a loop of its own in canvas.rs: an alpha from a table by squared distance, then the blend, about three times cheaper. Measured in release on perf.rs's 1,000 strokes at its 10 × 20 cells, a change of what's hovered is 0.44 ms (the disc's hover was 0.7) and a move 0.11 ms. With `dial_shot` at your 19 × 40 cells, where the dial is twice the size, a move costs 0.23 ms in stage one and 0.28 ms in stage two, frame and all, and a change of what's hovered 0.9 and 1.25 ms, or 1.8 ms on the ellipse, whose arc puts the readout lowest (after the review's fixes). Each event presents once, the dial's square, since the crosshair's strips span it, and the real sink unions the presents of one turn anyway.

1161. The renders: `extensions/die-whiteboard/examples/dial_shot.rs`, run as `cargo run --release -p die-whiteboard --example dial_shot -- DIR`, as die-markdown's `page_shot` is run. It drives a board of strokes, shapes, an arrow and labels in several inks through the harness and its fake pixel sink. It uses mono-dark's colours, copied, since an extension doesn't depend on your config, and your cells. Each frame is composited as the host does it: the cells' backgrounds, the pixel buffer, then the glyphs in IoskeleyMonoTerm through fonts.rs. It writes the frame and a crop around the dial for stage one with nothing hovered, on the pen, on arrow and in a corner; stage two on the pen in its first band, straight out, at ink 2 thick and at ink 2 thin, on text and on the ellipse; and the cell renderer's stage one and two. Then it prints the costs. I looked at each and changed what they showed. The scrim was too weak, the dead zone as loud as the ring, and the guides ran through names. A width label read as part of `select`. Text's needle had width ticks with no widths, the readout fell off the area's edge, and the preview dot hid under the chip. They're with this run's report, not in the tree.

1162. Tests. dial.rs's unit tests cover the ring, the stage-two thresholds and bands, the arc's placement by the armed ink, the clamping past its ends, text without a width, the eraser without a second stage, the readouts, and the names' clearance at 20 places in a cell. tests/dial.rs, rewritten where it held the disc and the reverse video: stage two arms and sets the ink and width, the click picks there, and stage one changes neither. A selection takes them as one entry, and the same again logs none. The cells screen's names, chip colours, readout, swatches and hidden names. The pixel renderer draws no disc but a scrim, the accent's stretch and dot, and a clear chip, presents the dial's square alone, and puts the picture back. The faces, and a theme restyling them. Moves that meant stage one stop at 27 units now, inside where stage two opens. perf.rs times a change of what's hovered and a move. die-dev: the dial through your config in mono-dark's grey and red.

1163. **For veto:** nobody has seen it on screen. It was checked through the harness's pixels and frames, composited into PNGs at your cell size and looked at one by one. fontdue's rasterizing of IoskeleyMonoTerm is close to die-render's but not the same. No test DIE drew it, and nothing went on your screen.

1164. No change to die-core or die-render. die-whiteboard is 5,961 lines, uncapped (an extension), 6,068 after the review's fixes; die-dev is 298 → 300.

1165. The gate on the first tree (1df374a):
- `cargo fmt --all --check`.
- `cargo xtask lint` (14 crates).
- `cargo xtask budget`: all within limits; die-core 15,859 of 15,860 and die-render untouched.
- `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --locked --offline -- -D warnings`.
- Under `DIE_UNSEEN=1`, `cargo test -p die-whiteboard`: 87 passed (29 unit, 15 dial, 2 export, 5 file, 5 keys, 1 perf, 13 pixels, 1 render, 2 session, 14 tools). `cargo test -p die-dev --test whiteboard --test theme --test looks --test config`: 22 passed, `looks`' one ignored as ever.

Not run: the rest of the workspace's tests, the ignored tests of `ci --full`, `ci --linux`, `mutate`, and S1's on-screen checks.

**die-whiteboard's dial: the review's fixes (2026-11-18)**

1166. Two reviewers sent nine findings. I checked each against the code, and the design ones against the renders too, trying to refute it. All nine held, and all are fixed. The first was the flick. One move could hover a tool and open its arc in the same call, so a flick that landed past the ring never went through stage one. The reviewer's case was a move 35 units out at 60° with two strokes selected. That's rect's sector, past where the arc opens, and in ink 2's segment of rect's arc. Letting go armed rect with ink 2, thin, and restyled both strokes. Now the arc opens only on a move after the one that hovered the tool in stage one, and a release (Space's or the right button's) never opens it; once it's open, the release moves in it as any move does. So that case arms rect alone and leaves the selection and the undo log as they were.

1167. **For veto:** the arc's first band picks nothing. A flick of a few moves still gets past the ring, so the band where the arc opens, from half a cell past the ring to a cell and a quarter further, is a margin. The arc, its guides and the chip show, but the needle stays dotted, the dot is the accent's, no segment is thick, and the readout says what stage one would (`rect: once`, `pen: ink 1 · medium`). Letting go there is stage one's. Past its guide, marked `2`, the ink and width are picked as before: thin, medium, thick. So that the dial stays its size, the bands are 1.25 cells deep, not 1.5: thin from 4.25 cells out, medium from 5.5, thick from 6.75, with the arc at 7.5. Text gets the one guide, unlabelled, where picking starts. This also fixes 1153's ink change and selection restyle, which keeping only the width (what 1153 had offered) would have left. Going back into the band un-picks without leaving the arc.

1168. **For veto:** the look, from the reviewers and the renders:
- Stage two's chip goes past the arc in the tool's direction, clear of its caps, and of the swatches in cells, as the reel's chip sits outside its value arc. Between the ring and the arc there's only the needle and the guides, so pushing straight out no longer runs the needle and the dot through `pen`. Its cells are solid, so the scrim doesn't grow for it, but the square drawn and cleared under it does.
- The `2` `4` `8` labels sat on the ring before: the `2` was half a cell past it, on the hovered stretch's end and its tick, in most places a centre can fall. Now each goes a cell and a half past its guide's counter-clockwise end, or on along the guide half a cell at a time, or past the other end. It goes where it's clear of the ring and its ticks, the arc with its ticks and caps, and the guides, and not within three cells of the chip or another label on its row. The first try read `4 2`. A unit test puts every inky tool, with inks 1, 4 and 6, at 20 places in a cell at both cell sizes, and checks each label against points on all the line art.
- The preview dot is a faint disc, not a ring (1154).
- A board label under the scrim dims whole, in both renderers, rather than cut mid-word (`the d` faint and `ial` bright).
- Each renderer lays out only what it draws: swatches for cells, width labels for pixels. A name gives way only to what's drawn there, so `select` and `eraser` no longer vanish beside nothing.

1169. Tests, renders, costs and the gate.
- New unit tests in dial.rs: a flick of one move, a release where it landed, a move into another sector past the ring, the first band, the labels and the chip as above, and what each renderer lays out.
- New harness tests in tests/dial.rs: the flick with a selection three ways (one move, a few into the first band, a right drag), leaving the styles, the selection and the undo log; the first band's accent dot; the thin preview's faint disc with no ring past it; a right drag far past the dial's square, to the area's corners (the pointer is held at the area's edge) and back, which leaves the buffer byte for byte as a straight drag there does, with Esc putting the picture back whole (with the restore of what lies outside the square taken out, it fails); and a label dimmed whole in both renderers.
- The tests that held the old bands, the cells' hidden names and the chip at the ring have the new numbers.
- I ran `dial_shot` again and looked at each shot: stage two on the pen in its first band, straight out, at ink 2 thin and thick, on text and on the ellipse; stage one idle, on the pen, on arrow and in a corner; and the cells' two. They're with this run's report.
- Costs in release: on perf.rs's 1,000 strokes, a change of what's hovered 0.44 ms and a move 0.11 ms, as before. `dial_shot` at your cells: a move 0.23 and 0.28 ms; the line art alone 0.89 ms in stage one, 1.25 ms in stage two, and 1.81 ms on the ellipse.

Under `DIE_UNSEEN=1`, `cargo test -p die-whiteboard` passed 93 (32 unit, 18 dial, 2 export, 5 file, 5 keys, 1 perf, 13 pixels, 1 render, 2 session, 14 tools), and `cargo test -p die-dev --test whiteboard --test theme --test looks --test config` passed 22, with `looks`' one ignored as ever.

Not run: the rest of the workspace's tests, the ignored tests of `ci --full`, `ci --linux`, `mutate`, and S1's on-screen checks. Nobody has seen it on screen (1163).

**Scratch boards: `\w` with no file to name first (2026-11-18)**

1170. What you asked: "we should be able to just open a scratch board with "\w" and then when we are ready to export/save then we choose the save location. Rather than trying to force the user to create an actual file first in the desired location. Many whiteboards will likely be throwaway and never need to be saved - a scratch space for whiteboarding". So `\w` (`whiteboard` with no file), from a buffer that isn't a board, goes to the board used last, a scratch board or a file's, or with none open makes a scratch board. From a board it makes another, so `\w\w` is a fresh one. No prompt, no file. `M-x whiteboard-new` always makes one. The old `whiteboard-new`, the `Board: ` prompt, is `M-x whiteboard-find` now. `:whiteboard FILE` is as it was.
1171. A scratch board is `*whiteboard: scratch*`, then `*whiteboard: scratch 2*`, the lowest number no buffer has, so a killed one's number is the next one's. Its directory is that of the buffer `\w` was typed in (from a board, the board's). Its status line is `scratch  pen·2  medium  12 elements`, never with ` *`, since no file is behind it. In the code it's a `BoardState` whose `path` is None. `dirty` is never set on one, so the autosave timer, the write at a kill and the write at a hand-over pass it by without a check of their own.
1172. **For veto:** killing a scratch board discards it without asking, as Emacs's `*scratch*` goes and as the brief has it, even with a thousand strokes on it. `:bd` asks nothing; `:q` and `ZQ` only close the window, as on any board. A board you'd miss is one `S` away. Emacs asks about a buffer that visits a file, and a scratch board visits none.
1173. A restart or quit keeps a scratch board in the session: its picture in the dialect (the file's format, so it reads back exactly), its number, its directory, and its view, tool, ink, width, selection and last export. It comes back under its own number, so `scratch 2` stays `scratch 2` with `scratch` gone. An empty one isn't kept. Nothing of it is on disk outside the session file.
1174. **For veto:** what a crash loses. A file board's unsaved changes at a crash are at most a second old, its autosave's delay. A scratch board has no autosave, so it's as old as core's last session write: a second after you stop, five at most while you keep drawing (`QUIET` and `MOST_BEHIND` in session.rs). I didn't give the whiteboard a timer of its own to push the session, since core's already runs.
1175. **For veto:** what keeping it costs. Core looks at the session twice a second while you work and asks each extension for its state each time, and writing a board of 1,000 strokes out as SVG takes about 3.5 ms in release. So the whiteboard keeps each scratch board's picture with the revision of the picture it was written at, and writes it again only when that has moved. Measured with `Ctx::session_json`, which also encodes (a look doesn't), in release on a scratch board of 1,000 strokes (600 KB in the session): 5.4 ms the first time, 0.7 ms unchanged, 3.2 ms after a change, against 0.25 ms with only an empty board. Drawing on a big scratch board costs a few ms twice a second on the main thread, about what a file board's autosave costs writing the same text once a second.
1176. `S` on a scratch board asks `Save board as: `. It's the `Board: ` prompt's descent (as `\f`'s), from the board's directory with `~` abbreviated, listing boards and directories. It shares `Board: `'s history, so a board saved this way lists first there next time. **For veto:** `<CR>` takes the name as typed, as Emacs's `write-file` does; a board the prompt lists is taken only once you move to it with `<Down>`, or put on the line with `<Tab>`. `Export to: ` does the same (1179). The review found that `\f`'s way, the best match chosen as you type, made `S arch RET` write over an `archive.svg` there (after a question that names it), and made a new `b.png` typed in `Export to: ` into the `board.png` exported last, written over with no question at all. What it costs: `<CR>` on part of a directory's name doesn't go into it; `<Tab>` or `<Down>` does. `Board: ` keeps `\f`'s way, since what it opens it doesn't write. **For veto:** a name not ending in `.svg` gets it: `arch` is `arch.svg`, and `diagram.png` is `diagram.png.svg`, where refusing it would have refused `notes.v2` too. The review found `diagram.png` kept as typed, a board's file holding SVG that `whiteboard-export png` then wrote a PNG over (now refused too, 1182), so the board couldn't come back. A directory answered asks again from there. Another open board's file is refused, by any path to it: `arch.svg is open in another board; save this one elsewhere`. A path is that board's file when it's the same file on disk (device and inode), reached through a link or, on macOS's disk that ignores case, as `ARCH.svg`; or, for a file not written yet, the same name in the same directory as the disk resolves it. The review found `ARCH` and a link let two boards share one file, each autosave writing over the other's. `:whiteboard FILE` goes to the board open on such a path the same way, where it opened a second board on the file. Once written it's a file board from then on: the path set, the buffer renamed `*whiteboard: NAME.svg*`, its directory the file's, written a second after each change. A write that fails leaves it a scratch board, with the error on the status line. `ZZ` asks the same, then closes the window, and a cancel closes nothing. `S` on a file board writes, as it did.
1177. **For veto:** a file there already asks `File ~/notes/old.svg exists; overwrite? (y or n) `. That's core's idiom for a yes or no, `y` or `yes` then `RET`, as `find-file`'s "create?" and Cmd-Q's question about a terminal take it, not Emacs's one-key `y-or-n-p`: core has no one-key read. Any other answer, or a cancel, writes nothing.
1178. Core: `Ctx::rename_buffer(buf, name) -> String`, as Emacs's `rename-buffer` with its unique flag. The name is made unique as a new buffer's is (`a<2>`), a buffer renamed to its own name keeps it, and a dead buffer is ignored and called "". It's 8 lines, with a test in `tests/workspace.rs`. die-core's limit goes from 15,860 to 15,870, with a note in budget.toml's style (15,867 measured). Its module, workspace, windows, pointer, stays under its 1,405 (1,399). And `PromptSpec::line_first`, for 1176: no row is chosen until you move to one, so `<CR>` answers with the line as typed, as `matches_part` already has it; `<Tab>`, `<Down>` and a directory's descent are as they were. It's 8 lines, with a test in `tests/prompt.rs`. die-core's limit goes on to 15,880 (15,875), and the prompt module's from 1,405 to 1,410 (1,409), each with a note.
1179. `E` asks `Export to: `, filled in with a path: for a file board `NAME.png` beside the file (today's default), for a scratch board `board.png` in its directory, or `board-2.png` and on if that's there. It's the same descending prompt as `Save board as: `, `<CR>` taking the line as it stands (1176), listing directories and PNG and SVG files, with a history of its own. The review found `E RET` on `plan.png` answered `plan-v1.png`, the file it flex matched, and asked to write over that. The answer's extension says what's written, in any case: `.png` the PNG export, `.svg` the plain SVG (what `NAME.export.svg` held). Anything else is refused: `Export to a .png or an .svg, not pic.txt`. A file there already asks to overwrite, unless it's this board's last export as the export left it (1180). `M-x whiteboard-export png|svg` writes beside a file board without asking, as before, unless that's an open board's own file (1182); on a scratch board it asks, filled in with that extension.
1180. **For veto:** `E` is filled in with where this board exported last, when it has, ahead of the brief's defaults. Otherwise a scratch board exported to `board.png` would be offered `board-2.png` at the next `E`, since `board.png` is taken by then, and exporting the same picture again would make a new file each time. With it, `E RET` again rewrites the same export without a question, while the file is as that export left it: its inode, length and modification time, taken when it's written and kept with the path. Once anything else has written it, as another board saved there and closed, or the picture changed in another program, `E` asks again. The review found it wrote plain SVG over a closed board's file there, which then came back as not a whiteboard file. The last export and its stamp are kept through a restart. For a file board it means an `E` to `~/exports/arch.png` is offered there again, not beside the file.
1181. **For veto:** a scratch board's SVG export is offered as `board.export.svg`, not `board.svg`. A `.svg` without `.export` is what `Board: ` lists as a board, and opening it is refused, `not a whiteboard file`. The name follows the file board's `NAME.export.svg`.
1182. **For veto:** an export to an open board's own file is refused, `f.svg is a board's own file; export to another`, by any path to it as 1176 has it, from `Export to: ` and from `whiteboard-export png|svg` alike, since the export would put a picture the board can't read back over its dialect. An export over a board file that isn't open only asks to overwrite, as any file does.
1183. `M-x whiteboard-open` on a scratch board writes its picture, in the dialect, to `$TMPDIR/die-whiteboard-PID-N.svg` and opens that. **For veto:** the temporary file stays, because the browser reads it after `open` returns and nothing tells DIE when it has; the system clears `$TMPDIR`. `C` works as it did, and its message names the board: `Copied scratch as a PNG`.
1184. **For veto:** a scratch board's directory is set to the one the command was given (the command's buffer's, or else the focused one's), not left to core's rule for a new buffer (the focused buffer's). The two agree for `\w`, and the command's is right for one run on another buffer.
1185. die-dev: `\w` is bound as it was, to `whiteboard`, and its comment says what that does now. The die-dev tests open a scratch board with `\w` and save it with `S`, open the file board through `:whiteboard talk`, and export with `E RET`.
1186. How it's built. `lib.rs`: `Whiteboard::scratch` and `open` share the buffer's making (`add`); the three prompts are one `ask_path` with a label, a history, a listing and whether it writes (`Board: `, `Save board as: `, `Export to: `); `unless_kept` asks before a file is replaced, and `exported_here` says when it needn't; `board_on` finds a board by `file::same_file`, and `not_a_board_file` refuses an export over one; `save_as` writes, renames and moves the directory; the session's record has `file` or `scratch`, `dir` and `svg`. `surface.rs`: `path` is an `Option`, with the scratch number, the last export and its stamp beside it, and `name()` and `changed()` know a scratch board; nothing else in the surface changed, so the dial's work beside this one doesn't meet it. `file.rs`: the names, `board_file`, `abbreviate`, `free_export`, `same_file`, `stamp`, and `exports` beside `entries`. die-whiteboard is 5,408 lines, uncapped, up from 5,034.
1187. Tests. die-whiteboard's `tests/scratch.rs` (10). `whiteboard` opens one with no prompt and no file in the notes' directory, and nothing is written by a stroke, three seconds or a kill; `scratch 2` from a board, the board used last from text, `whiteboard-new`, and the lowest number free after a kill. `S`: the prompt and its listing, `.svg` added, the rename, the directory, the status line, autosave from then on, the number taken back, `n` keeping a file and `y` writing over it, a path another board has refused, a directory answered asking again. `ZZ` cancelled, then answered. `E`: `board.png`, the PNG decoded, `E` again unasked, `whiteboard-export svg` asking, `board-2.png`, `.svg` plain, `.txt` refused, a board's own file refused, another's export asked about. `whiteboard-open`'s temporary file, through `Whiteboard::file_to_open`, so no browser opens. From the review: `S arch RET` beside `archive.svg`, `E RET` beside `arch-v1.png`, and a new `a.png` typed over the last export's line, each writing the name typed, unasked, with the other file as it was; `S` and `E` to an open board's file through a link to its directory, and as `ARCH` where the disk ignores case, refused, and `:whiteboard` through the link going to that board; `S diagram.png RET` writing `diagram.png.svg`, and `whiteboard-export png` refused on a board opened as `x.png`; the last export, written over by another board saved there and closed, asked about again. And 1175's cost, the cache proved by what's kept rather than by a time: a stroke taken away behind the revision's back is still in the session, and is gone once the revision moves. `tests/session.rs`: a scratch board back after a restart with its number, directory, tool, ink, width, selection and zoom, the empty one not, nothing written beside it; after a second with its directory, tool, ink, width and zoom, and its last export, which `E` offers and `RET` writes again unasked; then `S` and a third restart bringing it back as a file board. `file.rs`'s unit tests: the names, `board_file`, `abbreviate`, `free_export`, `exports`, `same_file` through a link and by the disk's case, and `stamp`. die-core's `tests/prompt.rs`: a `line_first` prompt answers with the line, a moved-to row, `<Tab>`'s completion and a directory's descent. The tests that asserted `\w`'s prompt and `E`'s `png or svg` use `whiteboard-find` and `Export to: ` now.
1188. **For veto:** nobody has seen it on screen. It was checked on the harness with die-vim, as cells, files and the session's JSON, under `DIE_UNSEEN`. What only a use tells: whether `\w` landing on the board used last, rather than a fresh one, is what you reach for from text (`\w\w` is the fresh one), and whether `board.png` is the name you'd want offered.
1189. The gate on this tree, after the review's fixes:

- `cargo xtask lint` (14 crates).
- `cargo xtask budget`: die-core 15,875 of 15,880, its workspace module 1,399 of 1,405 and its prompt module 1,409 of 1,410; die-render untouched; die-whiteboard 5,408, uncapped; die-dev 298.

**The dial and scratch boards together (2026-11-18)**

1190. The two parts were built apart, each reviewed and fixed, then merged on `m0/integration` up to `main`'s 232870d. The only conflict was this log, where both inserted their calls after 1146; both blocks stand, the dial's 1147–1169 then scratch boards' 1170–1189. `BoardState` took both sets of fields without a conflict (the dial's armed look, scratch boards' `path: Option`, `scratch`, `exported` and `exported_as`), as did `lib.rs`, the guide's whiteboard section and die-dev's whiteboard test. I looked at the dial's renders myself (stage one idle, on arrow and in a corner; stage two straight out, at ink 2 thick and on the ellipse; the cells' stage two): nothing overlaps but names cut at the area's edge (1157). The gate on the merged tree:
- `cargo fmt --all --check`; `cargo xtask lint` (14 crates); `cargo xtask budget` (exit 0: die-core 15,875 of 15,880; die-whiteboard 6,442, an extension; die-dev 300); `cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --locked --offline -- -D warnings`.

**die-fig: what you asked, the design study and your decisions (2026-11-21 and 22)**

1191. What you asked, on 2026-11-21: "I'd like to make another extensibility module ... designed around good graphing capabilities for generic figures, charts, graphs etc ... follow the ethos of my other tool fig". die-fig is that module: an extension under `extensions/`, as die-markdown and the whiteboard are, that draws figures from data in pixels. fig's ethos carries over. A figure is one JSON value tagged by its `type`, the data carries its own look in fig blocks (`color`, `size`, `show-label`, `override-focus`), and one set of controls works for every kind of figure. fig's own graph, line and histogram files open unchanged. Phases 0 to 2, logged here, make the chart: `\v` on a CSV, TSV, JSON, JSONL, fig or spec buffer shows it as a chart that follows the buffer as you type, with the keys and the mouse below. Network graphs, the detail panel, dashboards, commands as sources and the braille fallback are phases 3 to 5.
1192. The design study came before any code: https://claude.ai/artifact/1N6YhLovmXP91HabXHUUs6. A copy, `die-fig-design-study.html`, is in `die-fig-previews/2-design-mockups/` with its four mockups. It covers figures as data (fig's files, bare data with a guessed figure, and a small grammar of data, marks and encodings under both), the look (m1, a line chart with its cursor and card; m2, stacked bars hovered; m3, a network graph with its detail; m4, a dashboard), the controls, the mouse, speed, the modules, die-draw and D14, and five phases: foundations, the chart, graphs and detail, dashboards and live data, and the fallback and finish. For speed, each window has a base layer and an overlay, so a hover repaints only the overlay, and pan and zoom read a min/max pyramid, so a million points cost what the pixel columns cost.
1193. Your answers to the study, on 2026-11-21, were three decisions. Lift `extensions/die-draw` now, and amend D14 so an extension may depend on die-core and die-draw (1199). Keys put a cursor on the data, `h` `l` through points and `j` `k` through series, with `J` `K` kept as zoom aliases. A chart over files drives the first build. Then: "sounds great - Id also like mouse integrations as we would expect from a modern dashboard similar to how mouse interactivity is well done in the whiteboard module". The build's contract made that first-build scope: hover, the card, the brush, pan, wheel zoom, legend clicks, node drag, and a whiteboard-style dial on the right button and Space, which phase 1 ported.
1194. Your correction, on 2026-11-22, with phase 1 landed: "i dont know that we need radial dial features for the mouse selection stuff. I just meant we want mouse interaction as in beign able to hover over graphs and have interactive visualizations of lines and showing hovered values etc. as we would expect from a modern dashboard type application." So there's no dial in die-fig. The right button and Space do nothing on a figure, Ctrl-click is a plain click, and every action stays on its key. Hover is the heart of the mouse, to the standard of Grafana, Datadog and Observable. The crosshair follows the pointer's every sample and snaps to the nearest sample. A dot rings each series' value there, and one card lists every series' value at that x. The series nearest the pointer comes forward, the others fade, and its row in the card is marked. A bar under the pointer lifts, a legend key under the pointer brings its series forward, and everything clears when the pointer leaves. Then, as dashboards do: a brush zooms, the wheel pans, ⌘-wheel zooms about the pointer, a double click resets, a click puts the cursor, ⇧-click sets a reference with deltas, a legend click toggles and ⌥-click solos. The dial had been built, reviewed, fixed and landed (P1-dial, merged at a96694d). Phase 2's surface package removed all of it: `src/dial.rs` and `tests/dial.rs`, the scene's `DialSector`, `DialStage2` and `DialCentre` hits, `Theme.dial` and `Theme.dial_accent` with the `fig-dial` and `fig-dial-accent` faces and mono-dark's styles for them, the dial's gesture states, `OverlayState.dial`, and every mention in the docs. Its 19 calls are void and aren't listed here, and its renders are gone from your Desktop.
1195. **For veto:** the design study's own calls, which stand as built:
- The crate is die-fig and the extension `fig`. `\v` charts any buffer in normal state and a selection in visual state; `\g` is the terminal, and `\d`, `\s` and `\t` are taken in your init.el.
- fig's three file types open unchanged, and its colour names keep their meanings.
- The chrome takes mono-dark's faces. Series take eight new faces, `fig-series-1` to `-8`, in the study's order, with a light set for light themes. The four status colours are kept for status.
- The keyboard cursor's chip is inverted, light on dark, rather than your red accent, because red is a series and a status here. The accent marks only DIE's own affordances.
- No pie or donut charts, and no second y axis.
- Lines are 2 px, bars up to 24 px with a 4 px rounded end, and points 8 px with a ring of the background. Grids are horizontal hairlines.
- Times show in local time. CSV and date parsing are written here, with no new crates.
- PNG export is at 2× on the theme's background, as the whiteboard's is.
1196. **For veto:** the calls made while planning the build, also in the study:
- A spec that runs commands (`run`, `stream`), or reads a dashboard's panel files from outside its own folder, will ask first, naming each command, and remember your yes for that file and that command (phase 4). `:fig !cmd`, which you type yourself, asks nothing.
- Graph labels wear text colours, never a node's, unlike fig's coloured labels (phase 3).
- Past eight series on unstacked marks, the rest draw in one neutral grey and stay hoverable. The card lists twelve rows and then "+N more". "Other" exists only for stacked parts of a whole, through the `top` transform. This replaced the study's "a ninth series folds into Other".
- A colour belongs to its series. Slots are given in source order when the chart is built, and never move when series are hidden.
- No series is emphasised when a figure opens. The cursor appears at your first key, click or search hit.
- A restart brings back each figure that was focused, with its view: the zoom, the hidden series and the cursor. Split windows and the undo history aren't kept, because core drops surface windows at restore.
- Specs and JSON data go through die-fig's own JSON reader, so every error has a line and a column, and columns keep the file's order.
- Times are parsed and shown with jiff in the system's zone. A day-first or slashed date like 21/11/2026 needs an explicit `"format"`.
1197. **For veto:** the build contract's rulings that you'd see. The press that focuses a window only focuses it, for 150 ms (`FOCUS_GRACE`), as on a board. The y axis gets a value chip only on scatters, heatmaps and box brushes, so the overlay's pieces stay close and its damage one small rectangle. In a fig block, `size` 1, 2 or 3 is a line of 2, 3 or 4 px, a point of 8, 11 or 14 px, and a node's radius times 1, 1.4 or 1.8. `dash: true` is 6 px on and 4 off, `label` replaces the display name (on a rule it's the annotation), and `hidden: true` starts hidden. A `color` beats emphasis, `override-focus` matters only without a colour (fig's rule), and an absent `render_options` means true. A host without pixels gets one message, `fig: this host shows no pixels yet`, and no buffer, until phase 5's cells. Text never wears a series colour, except a label inside a filled mark, which is white or near black by the mark's luminance.
1198. How it was built. A skeleton (P0) landed straight on `m0/integration`, with every module of the design's tree and the contract's pinned types and signatures. Eight phase-1 packages followed, in parallel worktrees: die-draw, the core pointer slice, data, spec, scale, render, algo and the dial. Each was reviewed from two angles, fixed, and merged in one landing (0894d45). That was 34 agents. Phase 2 was two packages in parallel, the chart (P2-plot) and the surface (P2-surface, built against a stand-in of the chart). Adversarial reviewers went through each, and critics rendered PNGs of the chart and looked at them. Each was fixed, and both were merged with a die-dev round trip (d48009c). A look pass on the merged tree came last (ffa5c91, 668968e, ac12799). The contracts and the briefs (the mouse's interaction spec, the core API, how the whiteboard's pointer works, and a critic's list of pitfalls) are in the run's scratchpad, not the tree. What they settled is in the calls below and in the code's docs.

**die-fig phase 0: the skeleton, the wiring and D14 (2026-11-21)**

1199. **For veto:** D14 and D11 are amended, as you decided on 2026-11-21 (scope.md). D14 now reads that each extension depends on die-core and may depend on die-draw, the shared drawing crate under `extensions/` that depends on no DIE crate. Nothing in `crates/` may depend on either, and die-draw moves out at M4 with the extensions that use it. D11 keeps core without a drawing API: extensions bring their own rasterizer or share die-draw, which came under the rule of two. That answers call 1145's question, since die-fig needs die-markdown's type whole. `cargo xtask lint` holds the rule. `EXTENSION_DEPS` and `EXTENSION_DEV_DEPS` gain die-draw, die-draw may depend on none of DIE's crates (die-test-cap for its tests), and both new crates are neutral. The d14 test has seven errors now: die-draw on die-core and on die-vim fail, die-render on die-draw fails, and die-fig on die-draw passes (782ca55).
1200. budget.toml counts die-draw and die-fig as extensions, uncapped and outside guard 7. die-draw is 1,809 lines and die-fig 21,846 on the merged tree.
1201. No new external crate. Cargo.lock gains only die-draw and die-fig. die-fig uses tiny-skia, jiff, die-draw and serde_json, the last only for the session's values; die-draw uses tiny-skia, ttf-parser and png. die-test-cap and die-vim are die-fig's dev-dependencies from the start, so later packages didn't edit its Cargo.toml.
1202. die-dev: `Fig::default()` is in your config's extensions, `\v` is bound to `fig-toggle` in normal and visual state, and mono-dark styles fig's chrome but not its series or statuses. fig-grid is `#2a2a2a`, written inline since no mono-dark constant has it; fig-baseline and fig-ring are the border's grey, fig-tick the mid grey, fig-text and fig-strong the text and bright greys, fig-tooltip the subtle ground, and fig-cursor bright. **For veto:** every fig face carries its colour as its foreground, fig-tooltip's and fig-cursor's too, though they paint grounds, as the whiteboard's dial faces do. die-dev is 311 lines, from 300.
1203. die-dev's tests/orchid.rs registers `Fig::default()`. It builds its harness from a hand-picked list of extensions, and your config's global `\v` fails at startup if `fig-toggle` doesn't exist. P0 also wrote die-dev's tests/fig.rs, which the gate named but which didn't exist: the config's extension list, `\v` in normal and visual state, `\hk` describing `\v`, mono-dark styling fig's chrome and not its series or statuses, and no timer before `\v`.
1204. **For veto:** the JSON reader is strict RFC 8259. A raw tab or other control character in a string is an error, a raw line break in a string is "unterminated string" at the opening quote, 1e400 is "number out of range", nesting past 256 levels is "nested too deeply", and a leading byte order mark is skipped. Besides the contract's five messages it says "expected ',' or ']'", "expected ':'", "expected a string key" (also for a trailing comma in an object), "expected a value" (also for a trailing comma in an array), "invalid escape", "invalid \u escape", "lone surrogate in \u escape", "control character in string" and "unexpected text after the value". Every error has its line, its column in characters, and its byte. Integers past 2^53 lose precision, as f64 does. `Node::to_text`, which `:fig-spec` writes with, writes whole numbers up to 2^53 with no fraction and others in Rust's shortest form (1e300, 1e-6).
